Gap = Support − Importance (1–5). Status is the official support status: ▼ Needs attention (Under Supported) · ● On track (Target) · ▲ Exceeds needs (Over Supported). Needs-attention items carry a severity from the rounded gap (−0.1 to −0.4 slight, −0.5 to −0.9 moderate, −1.0 or worse severe) and are marked borderline when the gap's 95% interval includes 0; “On track, low support” = on track but Support under 60/100. Digital & Tech has no Importance rating: ◇ shows its agreement band (Strong ≥ 4.0 · Moderate 3.5–3.9 · Needs attention < 3.5).

Top priorities — survey items

The five survey items with the highest Friction at this site, with the comment themes linked to each and one illustrative comment.

1Availability of spaces that support discretion▼ Needs attention · moderate
  • Friction 28/100 · 51% under-supported (Physical Feature)
  • Comment themes: Privacy & Confidentiality — 162 people (23% of respondents)
My team is virtual and collogues are vitural. Coming into the pulse point does not support my ability to work with my virtual team or collogues. Due to the nature of the work (PHI and privacy), I attempt to plan those types of discussions/activities outside of the days I am working in the office.Director or Manager · comment tagged Privacy & Confidentiality
2Focused individual work that requires concentration and minimal interruption▼ Needs attention · moderate
  • Friction 26/100 · 54% under-supported (Work Activity)
  • Comment themes: Focus / Concentration — 66 people (10% of respondents); Noise & Distraction — 160 people (23% of respondents); Space Availability & Crowding — 67 people (10% of respondents)
There is no reason for me to be in an office. The people i work with live in different parts of the country so I sit in a corner by myself and talk on the phone to those people. It is inefficient, distracting, and wastes my time commutingAssociate · comment tagged Noise & Distraction

The floor has too many personal conversations going on. This does not mean people are talking about their personal lives, they are talking about members and people on the phone can hear and it's very uncomfortable when taking escalated calls.Associate · comment tagged Noise & Distraction

5Tasks requiring discretion▼ Needs attention · moderate

I think the biggest thing is being able to overhear other conversations while they are in meetings. I fear that conversations that are meant to be private can be overheard.Associate · comment tagged Privacy & Confidentiality

Operational Action Plan — What to Do Next
Auto-generated draft to validate — not a re-summary of the survey. Primary owner for every action: Regional Director + Local Team. A full read of the report card below is still recommended. The team's job: Confirm / Modify / Reject, then assign owner, cost, and timing.
Evidence check (2026-11-10): the corrected data weakens the support for the items below. They are left in place for the site team to confirm, modify or reject.
  • Priority 2 (Desk reservations and booking window): the two-week booking window it centres on is mentioned in 11 comments from 9 people; the figure it cited (375 comments) counted every mention of a desk. After the re-tag Desk Availability & Reservations is still raised by 147 people (21% of respondents), so desk booking remains material, but the booking-window change itself rests on a small number of comments — validate locally first.
  • Protect item 'Community & Events': the true positive share is 45% (20% negative) — it no longer clears the What's-working bar
1Private space for sensitive meetings and all-day callsLOCAL PROJECT
Associates on sensitive-topic meetings lack all-day private space, and meeting-heavy calendars make the quiet zones hard to actually use.
What this looks like here
  • Lack of offices in which I can work all day. I am on meetings on sensitive topics, and working in general areas is not appropriate.
  • Too much noise and distraction at times. So many meetings on our calendar it's hard to utilize the Quiet Zones.
Evidence
  • Finding Focused / Private Work is the #1 priority category (82/100); discretion-space friction 28/100 (51% under-supported).
  • Finding Privacy & Confidentiality: 162 people (23% of respondents), 224 comments, 64% negative; Noise & Distraction: 160 people (23% of respondents), 205 comments, 67% negative.
Proposed action Assess enclosed/quiet-space capacity against the meeting-heavy demand pattern; review whether quiet zones are being absorbed by meetings and need protection or expansion.
Team Primary: Regional Director + Local Team · Support: Workplace Solutions, Change & Communications
Site team must Measure private-room demand vs. availability across a typical week and escalate the capacity gap with evidence.
2Desk reservations and booking windowWORKSTREAM / ENTERPRISE
The two-week booking window is too short for how associates plan, and reservations are the highest-volume complaint at the largest site.
What this looks like here
  • Reservations should be available more than 2 weeks out. It is very inconvenient that we can only schedule 2 weeks at a time.
Evidence
  • Finding Desk Availability & Reservations: 147 people (21% of respondents), 186 comments, 48% negative — Physical Environment is the #2 priority category (76/100).
Proposed action Review the booking-window policy (is the 2-week limit local or enterprise?) and no-show handling.
Team Primary: Regional Director + Local Team · Support: Reservation-system owner, Technology
Site team must Confirm whether the 2-week window is a local setting or enterprise policy, and route accordingly.
3Workstation equipment and connectivity reliabilityLOCAL PROJECT
Monitors and video often don't work, and USB-C cords on specific floors fail to connect to the network — the site's Digital & Tech score is the lowest of all sites.
What this looks like here
  • Often the monitors don't work and occasionally the video meetings don't work.
  • Many of the USBC cords don't work and allow you to connect to the network on the 4th floor.
Evidence
  • Finding Hardware: 176 people (26% of respondents), 237 comments, 61% negative; Connectivity: 131 people (19% of respondents), 162 comments, 57% negative.
  • Finding Digital & Tech dimension is 69.9 ± 1, below the company average (72.0).
Proposed action Run a floor-by-floor equipment and cable audit (the 4th-floor USB-C issue is specific and testable) and route confirmed faults to Technology.
Team Primary: Regional Director + Local Team · Support: Technology
Site team must Audit cords/monitors by floor, replace failed cables, and escalate the connectivity pattern to Technology with locations.
4Thermal comfort (temperature)LOCAL ACTION
The office is widely described as too cold — desks beneath supply vents especially — and it is one of the most frequent and most negative complaints at the site.
What this looks like here
  • It is always too cold in the office which makes it uncomfortable. …
  • The temperature is always frigid and even though I bring a jacket or sweater to wear, my hands are so cold it is difficult to even type. …
  • …The desks beneath a vent can be freezing cold & I'm typically hot.
Evidence
  • Finding Temperature / Thermal: 117 people (17% of respondents), 130 comments, 77% negative.
  • Finding Thermal / temperature comfort: Under Supported — friction 23/100 (46% under-supported).
Proposed action Have Workplace Operations map cold zones — starting with desks under supply vents — and check set-points, airflow balancing and diffuser direction in those zones.
Team Primary: Regional Director + Local Team · Support: Workplace Operations
Site team must Log cold zones and times by floor for two weeks, submit them to building operations, and verify the corrections hold.
5Chair comfort and sit/stand desk repairsLOCAL ACTION
Chairs are uncomfortable and hard to re-adjust at each new desk, and some standing desks in offices are broken.
What this looks like here
  • The chairs are very uncomfortable and hurt my back. …
  • I have back issues and adjusting the chairs each time in a new cubicle is not easy
  • The standing desks in some of the offices are broken.
Evidence
  • Finding Furniture & Ergonomics: 56 people (8% of respondents), 58 comments, 64% negative.
  • Finding Furniture comfort & ergonomics: Under Supported — friction 14/100 (34% under-supported).
Proposed action Audit chair and sit/stand desk condition, fix broken standing desks, and post simple chair-adjustment guidance at shared desks; assess a chair upgrade only if the audit shows widespread wear.
Team Primary: Regional Director + Local Team · Support: Workplace Operations, Workplace Solutions
Site team must Complete the chair and desk audit by floor, repair what is broken, and report the scale of any remaining gap.
Protect what is working
  • Reception & Entry — 53% positive and 18% negative across 45 comments from 42 people Maintain the current service level and monitor performance.
  • Community & Events — 45% positive and 20% negative across 40 comments from 36 people Maintain the current service level and monitor performance.
  • Wellbeing / Wellness Spaces — 55% positive and 18% negative across 22 comments from 20 people — the refreshed building is praised Maintain the current service level and monitor performance.

I understand that being person allows for collaboration, but there is not a very good place for you to take calls and avoid background noise. We added the quiet space, but that area cannot be used for phone calls. I am needing to find an area that limits conversations to meetings to generate a quiet location that does not pick up excessive background noise. — Director or Manager

Their participation is distracting. Enough of our team work remotely or in other facilities that being in-person for meetings isn't realistic or a benefit. — Associate

Having to transition back and forth from cubicles to available private spaces costs me a lot of time during my day. While I completely understand and support the goals of collaborating with associates across organizations within the company, it's challenging to accommodate the sensitive nature of some of the work we do when we are surrounded by associates who should not be privy to that information at that time. It would be helpful to have more 'control' over who sits in the HR neighborhood area to support that business need. — Director or Manager

There needs to be more hush booths or places where an individual can go to have private meetings or virtual meetings with their team that others cannot hear them. — Director or Manager
  • Especially in the DPAIO area, our teams are split across the country and the world - it is rare that people on the same direct or even indirect teams share a state, let alone a specific office and time of day. With the office having limited focus areas and monitor arrangements that are not conducive to development, data analytics, and other DPAIO SME workloads, time spent in office is generally the least productive. Travel time and expense is incurred to sit in the same Teams meeting as before, but with a non-optimized setup, lots of background noise, and constant focus-breaks. On the rare occasions when there is an in-person meeting or event that is possible, it is then often difficult to book an appropriate size and location conference room, and there's more complexity in setting up screenshare and other collaboration. In short - while it is designed reasonably for typical office work, it is not designed for typical development /DPAIO work, and thus is a net productivity loss for many members of my team. Fortunately much of leadership is in Indianapolis, so there is value in being able to meet in person for me on that aspect, and to be able to contribute in non-development activities - but outside of those scenarios, it is a net loss. — Associate
  • The floor has too many personal conversations going on. This does not mean people are talking about their personal lives, they are talking about members and people on the phone can hear and it's very uncomfortable when taking escalated calls. — Associate
  • I wish we had a few more quiet capules for meetings. Sometimes it's hard to find an open one nowadays. — Director or Manager
  • Those who are not Staff VP or above cannot reserve offices nor can they reserve a conference room space on a regular basis. When your position requires you to be on back-to-back calls as well as periodically presenting to external clients and consultants, the open concept is not ideal. Noise carries and actually sounds louder to the folks on the call versus what I can hear through headphones. There is no time and it is a hassle to try to move logistically from place to place to take a call. Many discussions are not something you want everyone to overhear. — Director or Manager
  • There are times when I need to share my screen and speak to topics that are confidential. My current job level does not allow me to reserve the single space offices that are on my floor right behind me. Those spaces should not be restricted to a certain job level. They're already restricted to 2 hour intervals, but anyone should be able to reserve those spaces. — Associate
  • 1:1's with my virtual associates (ALL my associates are virtual), especially if I have to deliver difficult news. There are not enough private areas to have these meetings. Also, my peers and associates complain that when I am in the office, they can hear a lot of background noise and that is disruptive. — Director or Manager
  • Directors having more flexibility & availability to secure a 'closed door' working space AHEAD of the day of and for MORE than 2 hours at a time. I simply cannot have 1x1 discussions out in the open 'cube farm' area. Everyone I meet with 1x1 with has something confidential in nature to discuss + in the midst this time of significant change within our overall organization I regularly have to leave my cube and find a place I can step into to finish a confidential, sensitive - or not yet formally communicated decision. This is a significant pain point for me. I'm in the office 3 days per week (Monday/Wednesday/Friday) and I am not able to schedule 1x1s and these confidential discussions/meetings only on Tuesday/Thursday when I work from home. — Director or Manager
  • The combination of limited staff vp office reservations, along with limited capacity (and poor circulation for long periods) of phone booths make it very challenging to be on calls more than 1 time per day without disturbing others in the vicinity by being on long calls from one's reserved desk. Working on confidential information (eg, employee information) is incredibly difficult given the 'open' layout where monitors of virtually every desk are viewable by others. — Director or Manager
  • as a recruiter at Elevance Health it is very difficult to perform on video screening meetings with candidates onsite. If I try to do it at my reserved spot, there is typically other talking and conversation in the background that can be disruptive/distracting. When I go to sit in the quiet booths to do these video screenings, it is difficult as I do not have a full keyboard or additional screens for ease of notes taking. — Associate
  • As an onsite Staff VP, I have the benefit of a personal office and can shut my door when I need to. My team mostly comes in together on Wednesdays.   • As a manager, one of my primary concerns with the current office layout is the limited ability to have confidential conversations with team members. Conducting monthly one-on-one meetings often requires me to coordinate around my remote work schedule because I can frequently provide a more private setting at home than in the office. This creates additional scheduling challenges and can impact the effectiveness of those discussions. While the office design is visually appealing and promotes an open environment, I do not feel it fully supports the nature of the work many of us perform. The current neighborhood model can create significant distractions, as employees from very different departments and job functions are often seated in close proximity. As a result, it is common to hear lengthy conversations or calls that are unrelated to my work, making it difficult to maintain focus throughout the day. In the previous office configuration, workspaces were more intentionally aligned with job functions. For example, call center teams, managers, and business teams were generally located in separate areas, which helped balance collaboration needs with concentration and noise management. The combination of higher cubicle walls and white-noise systems also provided a greater sense of privacy and reduced distractions. While the designated quiet zones are a valuable resource, they are not always a practical solution for employees who have a mix of focused work and meetings throughout the day. Frequently relocating between workspaces can be disruptive and reduce productivity. I would encourage consideration of additional privacy and noise-reduction measures, such as higher workstation partitions, expanded sound-masking solutions, more dedicated spaces for confidential conversations, or a reassessment of neighborhood assignments based on work type. These changes could help create an environment that better supports both collaboration and focused, productive work. — Director or Manager

  • One of the primary challenges with working in the PulsePoint office is the current workspace layout. In my role, I spend the majority of my day on conference calls. While some members of my team do come into the office, we rarely have opportunities to collaborate in person because much of our work involves virtual meetings with teams that are located across different PulsePoints and geographic locations. Another significant challenge is maintaining confidentiality during sensitive discussions. Currently, office space reservations are limited to two hours per day, which creates difficulties for managers and directors who are frequently involved in confidential meetings, employee discussions, and business-related conversations that require privacy. The limited availability of private workspace often makes it challenging to effectively perform these responsibilities while onsite. As a result, many of these meetings are now scheduled on days when I am working remotely, which can create additional scheduling and collaboration challenges. Providing managers and directors with the ability to reserve private office space for longer periods of time would greatly improve productivity, support confidentiality requirements, and enhance the overall in-office experience. Expanding access to private workspaces would allow leaders to be more effective while onsite and would better align the office environment with the realities of how many teams operate today. — Director or Manager

  • As a manager, one of my primary concerns with the current office layout is the limited ability to have confidential conversations with team members. Conducting monthly one-on-one meetings often requires me to coordinate around my remote work schedule because I can frequently provide a more private setting at home than in the office. This creates additional scheduling challenges and can impact the effectiveness of those discussions. While the office design is visually appealing and promotes an open environment, I do not feel it fully supports the nature of the work many of us perform. The current neighborhood model can create significant distractions, as employees from very different departments and job functions are often seated in close proximity. As a result, it is common to hear lengthy conversations or calls that are unrelated to my work, making it difficult to maintain focus throughout the day. In the previous office configuration, workspaces were more intentionally aligned with job functions. For example, call center teams, managers, and business teams were generally located in separate areas, which helped balance collaboration needs with concentration and noise management. The combination of higher cubicle walls and white-noise systems also provided a greater sense of privacy and reduced distractions. While the designated quiet zones are a valuable resource, they are not always a practical solution for employees who have a mix of focused work and meetings throughout the day. Frequently relocating between workspaces can be disruptive and reduce productivity. I would encourage consideration of additional privacy and noise-reduction measures, such as higher workstation partitions, expanded sound-masking solutions, more dedicated spaces for confidential conversations, or a reassessment of neighborhood assignments based on work type. These changes could help create an environment that better supports both collaboration and focused, productive work. — Director or Manager

  • One of the primary challenges with working in the PulsePoint office is the current workspace layout. In my role, I spend the majority of my day on conference calls. While some members of my team do come into the office, we rarely have opportunities to collaborate in person because much of our work involves virtual meetings with teams that are located across different PulsePoints and geographic locations. Another significant challenge is maintaining confidentiality during sensitive discussions. Currently, office space reservations are limited to two hours per day, which creates difficulties for managers and directors who are frequently involved in confidential meetings, employee discussions, and business-related conversations that require privacy. The limited availability of private workspace often makes it challenging to effectively perform these responsibilities while onsite. As a result, many of these meetings are now scheduled on days when I am working remotely, which can create additional scheduling and collaboration challenges. Providing managers and directors with the ability to reserve private office space for longer periods of time would greatly improve productivity, support confidentiality requirements, and enhance the overall in-office experience. Expanding access to private workspaces would allow leaders to be more effective while onsite and would better align the office environment with the realities of how many teams operate today. — Director or Manager

  • As a manager, one of my primary concerns with the current office layout is the limited ability to have confidential conversations with team members. Conducting monthly one-on-one meetings often requires me to coordinate around my remote work schedule because I can frequently provide a more private setting at home than in the office. This creates additional scheduling challenges and can impact the effectiveness of those discussions. While the office design is visually appealing and promotes an open environment, I do not feel it fully supports the nature of the work many of us perform. The current neighborhood model can create significant distractions, as employees from very different departments and job functions are often seated in close proximity. As a result, it is common to hear lengthy conversations or calls that are unrelated to my work, making it difficult to maintain focus throughout the day. In the previous office configuration, workspaces were more intentionally aligned with job functions. For example, call center teams, managers, and business teams were generally located in separate areas, which helped balance collaboration needs with concentration and noise management. The combination of higher cubicle walls and white-noise systems also provided a greater sense of privacy and reduced distractions. While the designated quiet zones are a valuable resource, they are not always a practical solution for employees who have a mix of focused work and meetings throughout the day. Frequently relocating between workspaces can be disruptive and reduce productivity. I would encourage consideration of additional privacy and noise-reduction measures, such as higher workstation partitions, expanded sound-masking solutions, more dedicated spaces for confidential conversations, or a reassessment of neighborhood assignments based on work type. These changes could help create an environment that better supports both collaboration and focused, productive work. — Director or Manager

  • Indy's PulsePoint offers great amenities overall, and I appreciate the investment that has been made in creating the space. One challenge, however, is that the in-office experience is very different depending on whether you have access to a dedicated office or closer parking options. For most of us, much of the day is spent in virtual meetings. Since offices below staff vp level can only be reserved same day and for two-hour increments, it is difficult to plan ahead for days that require extensive calls, collaboration, or focused work. Working from the open cubicle space is challenging due to the level of activity and noise and often requires wearing a headset continuously throughout the day. After many consecutive hours of meetings, wearing either over-ear or in-ear headsets is physically uncomfortable. While phone booths can help with noise and privacy, they are not a practical alternative for extended work because workspace and monitor options are limited. Many meetings require multiple screens, which means associates need to bring and set up their own second monitor if they plan to work from a phone booth. In addition, on days with back-to-back meetings, finding an available phone booth between calls can be difficult and can result in being late to meetings while searching for an open space. The walk from the parking garage to the building also adds a meaningful amount of time to the commute. On lower-utilization office days, it would be helpful to explore opportunities for closer parking access from both a safety and time-management perspective. Additional flexibility around workspace reservations and parking would help make time in the office more productive and effective. I appreciate this survey as a way to share feedback! — Director or Manager

One enhancement that could make the office more compelling for employees in similar roles would be the addition of individual collaboration rooms or dedicated virtual-work spaces designed for a single person. These spaces could provide a more effective environment for leading virtual meetings, collaborating remotely, and focusing on deep work, creating a differentiated experience from a traditional workstation or home office. — Director or Manager

  • As a manager, one of my primary concerns with the current office layout is the limited ability to have confidential conversations with team members. Conducting monthly one-on-one meetings often requires me to coordinate around my remote work schedule because I can frequently provide a more private setting at home than in the office. This creates additional scheduling challenges and can impact the effectiveness of those discussions. While the office design is visually appealing and promotes an open environment, I do not feel it fully supports the nature of the work many of us perform. The current neighborhood model can create significant distractions, as employees from very different departments and job functions are often seated in close proximity. As a result, it is common to hear lengthy conversations or calls that are unrelated to my work, making it difficult to maintain focus throughout the day. In the previous office configuration, workspaces were more intentionally aligned with job functions. For example, call center teams, managers, and business teams were generally located in separate areas, which helped balance collaboration needs with concentration and noise management. The combination of higher cubicle walls and white-noise systems also provided a greater sense of privacy and reduced distractions. While the designated quiet zones are a valuable resource, they are not always a practical solution for employees who have a mix of focused work and meetings throughout the day. Frequently relocating between workspaces can be disruptive and reduce productivity. I would encourage consideration of additional privacy and noise-reduction measures, such as higher workstation partitions, expanded sound-masking solutions, more dedicated spaces for confidential conversations, or a reassessment of neighborhood assignments based on work type. These changes could help create an environment that better supports both collaboration and focused, productive work. — Director or Manager

  • As a manager, one of my primary concerns with the current office layout is the limited ability to have confidential conversations with team members. Conducting monthly one-on-one meetings often requires me to coordinate around my remote work schedule because I can frequently provide a more private setting at home than in the office. This creates additional scheduling challenges and can impact the effectiveness of those discussions. While the office design is visually appealing and promotes an open environment, I do not feel it fully supports the nature of the work many of us perform. The current neighborhood model can create significant distractions, as employees from very different departments and job functions are often seated in close proximity. As a result, it is common to hear lengthy conversations or calls that are unrelated to my work, making it difficult to maintain focus throughout the day. In the previous office configuration, workspaces were more intentionally aligned with job functions. For example, call center teams, managers, and business teams were generally located in separate areas, which helped balance collaboration needs with concentration and noise management. The combination of higher cubicle walls and white-noise systems also provided a greater sense of privacy and reduced distractions. While the designated quiet zones are a valuable resource, they are not always a practical solution for employees who have a mix of focused work and meetings throughout the day. Frequently relocating between workspaces can be disruptive and reduce productivity. I would encourage consideration of additional privacy and noise-reduction measures, such as higher workstation partitions, expanded sound-masking solutions, more dedicated spaces for confidential conversations, or a reassessment of neighborhood assignments based on work type. These changes could help create an environment that better supports both collaboration and focused, productive work. — Director or Manager

  • Indy's PulsePoint offers great amenities overall, and I appreciate the investment that has been made in creating the space. One challenge, however, is that the in-office experience is very different depending on whether you have access to a dedicated office or closer parking options. For most of us, much of the day is spent in virtual meetings. Since offices below staff vp level can only be reserved same day and for two-hour increments, it is difficult to plan ahead for days that require extensive calls, collaboration, or focused work. Working from the open cubicle space is challenging due to the level of activity and noise and often requires wearing a headset continuously throughout the day. After many consecutive hours of meetings, wearing either over-ear or in-ear headsets is physically uncomfortable. While phone booths can help with noise and privacy, they are not a practical alternative for extended work because workspace and monitor options are limited. Many meetings require multiple screens, which means associates need to bring and set up their own second monitor if they plan to work from a phone booth. In addition, on days with back-to-back meetings, finding an available phone booth between calls can be difficult and can result in being late to meetings while searching for an open space. The walk from the parking garage to the building also adds a meaningful amount of time to the commute. On lower-utilization office days, it would be helpful to explore opportunities for closer parking access from both a safety and time-management perspective. Additional flexibility around workspace reservations and parking would help make time in the office more productive and effective. I appreciate this survey as a way to share feedback! — Director or Manager

I would also like to share feedback regarding the current hybrid work requirements. While I understand the organization's goals in increasing in-office presence, my experience has been that I was more productive and maintained a better work-life balance when working remotely the majority of the time and coming into the office one day per week. The flexibility of that arrangement allowed me to incorporate healthy habits, such as taking a walk during lunch breaks, which improved my focus, energy level, and overall well-being. The current requirement to commute to the office three days per week has reduced that flexibility and added additional strain. My wife and I care for our non-verbal son with special needs, and the additional commute time and reduced flexibility can add stress to an already demanding caregiving schedule. 690 respondents at this site; counts under 5 print as “<5” with the percentage left blank.

Data source & method. PulsePoint survey export · completed responses · 10 in-scope sites. Support responses map to 0–100; each dimension is the average of its items; the Overall PulsePoint Experience Index is the unweighted average of the four Dimension Scores — the same calculation as the Tableau deck. ± is the 95% margin from resampling this site's respondents (2,000 resamples). Friction and status per the official Tableau spec. Theme prevalence counts people (one person can answer five open questions); comment counts are secondary. Themes come from a keyword taxonomy (29 themes across 7 categories, taxonomy.py). A comment shows the writer's role only when at least 10 respondents at the site share it; tenure is never shown beside a comment. “Company” means the 10 in-scope sites pooled, in the selected population (completed responses or all responses). Comment themes and sentiment are automated and not yet validated.
